What Bitcoin Did · Peter McCormack 1 episode(s) · 52 insights

The Universe Didn’t Come From Nothing | Brian Keating100 min52 insights2026-07-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Despite airing on a Bitcoin podcast, this episode contains essentially zero Bitcoin or crypto analysis — it is a cosmologist (experimental physicist, Simons Observatory / BICEP lead) walking the host through the physics of time, the Big Bang and the inflation-field hunt, then pivoting into an unusually specific bear case on AI: that LLMs on GPUs are architecturally incapable of superintelligence, that OpenAI's ~$800B valuation and ~$10B/month burn look like a dot-com-style bubble, and that the real risk is human dependency on tools whose funding could crater. Secondary market-adjacent threads: big-science capex and funding cutbacks (a ~$100M+ instrument, a cancelled US South Pole competitor, UK co-funding), and a diagnosis of collapsing public trust in science, government and UAP 'disclosure'.
Time as unsolved physics · 5Big Bang origins and the inflation hunt · 8Big-science capex, funding cuts and competitive position · 9Why LLMs on GPUs won't reach superintelligence · 11AI bubble, dependency and valuation risk · 7Trust collapse: Fauci, UAP disclosure, private epistemologies · 7Attention scarcity, meaning and the innocence thesis · 5
Time as unsolved physics 5
Experimental cosmologist Brian Keating argues physics has no working definition of time — space, matter and energy are understood, but time's arrow is not — and calls this the central embarrassment of his field.
thesis🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highcosmologyphysicstime
So what: Frames where frontier physics capital and talent are heading next: measurement of time's origin rather than more particle-collider energy.
“the reason is because there is no time. Time doesn't exist.” ▶ 0:05
The laws of physics are time-invariant — a pendulum, a bowling ball or an orbiting electron obey identical laws forwards and backwards — so nothing in fundamental physics mandates that time has a direction.
framework🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highphysicstime
So what: The mental model: an observed regularity with no underlying law is a research opportunity, not settled science — the same test he applies to AI claims later.
Keating rejects the standard entropy explanation for time's arrow: entropy can be locally reduced and the coffee-and-milk example is only irreversible at a chosen scale, so the second law is necessary but not sufficient to explain time's direction.
contrarian🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highthermodynamicsentropy
So what: Signals that a widely-repeated textbook explanation is considered inadequate by working experimentalists — useful calibration on how much of 'settled' science is provisional.
Keating's active research programme is a hypothesised 'cosmological time field' — a separate cosmological arrow of time, distinct from microphysics, that would encode the rate of time's flow and is claimed to be observable.
thesis🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: mediumSimons Observatorycosmology
So what: A concrete, falsifiable target for the next decade of cosmology instrumentation spend; watch Simons Observatory publications for a claimed detection.
Keating says the cosmological time-field signal has already been claimed as detected at a low confidence level, and his collaboration is aiming to make the definitive measurement.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: mediumSimons Observatory
So what: A low-confidence prior detection plus a dedicated instrument is the classic setup for a headline physics result — or a second retraction.
Big Bang origins and the inflation hunt 8
Keating's core research question is what the universe 'banged out of' — a pre-existing universe, or a static energy field that existed for all time — because something cannot truly come from nothing.
thesis🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highBig BanginflationBICEPSimons Observatory
So what: Defines the specific scientific question a $100M-class instrument is being built to answer; a positive result would be a generational physics headline.
“something can't truly come from nothing” ▶ 0:13
Keating explicitly rejects Lawrence Krauss's 'universe from nothing' position on the grounds that nature's laws have no agency — equations describe phenomena but do not instantiate them, just as Newton described rather than discovered gravity.
contrarian🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highLawrence KraussIsaac Newtonphysics
So what: The map-is-not-the-territory framework he later reuses to argue language models describe intelligence without instantiating it.
Physics can reconstruct the universe back to roughly one trillionth of a second after the Big Bang, at which point known physics — and confident extrapolation — breaks down.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highLarge Hadron ColliderBig Bangphysics
So what: Sets the concrete boundary of current knowledge that new instruments are being funded to push past.
The age of the universe has gone from 100% uncertainty in the 1990s (10 vs 20 billion years) to 13.826 billion years measured to roughly one part in 100,000 today.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highcosmology
So what: A precision improvement of five orders of magnitude in one career — evidence that instrument-driven measurement, not theory, has driven cosmology's returns.
“Now we know it 13.826 billion years with an uncertainty of about one in the last decimal place.” ▶ 27:31
Detecting primordial gravitational waves would not prove inflation but would eliminate every competing origin model — eternal universe, cyclic universe, black-hole endpoint — because none of them produce those waves.
causal🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highinflationBig Banggravitational waves
So what: Explains why a single measurement carries outsized scientific value: it is a one-shot elimination of the entire competing hypothesis space.
“if we see the waves of gravity, it's strong a circumstantial evidence for inflation, but b it kills off every other contender.” ▶ 45:41
Science proceeds by falsification not proof — Keating frames his own programme as an attempt to falsify the claim that there was no Big Bang, the same epistemic status as disproving flat-earth claims.
framework🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highscientific method
So what: The falsification standard is the benchmark he uses to dismiss UAP claims and AI-doom claims alike later in the conversation.
“So what we are trying to do is falsify the claim that there was no big bang.” ▶ 30:08
Keating claims dark matter has in fact already been detected in the form of neutrinos — massive elementary particles that do not interact with light — even though their abundance is insufficient to explain all observed phenomena.
contrarian🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highdark matterneutrinosphysics
So what: Cuts against the popular 'scientists don't know what 95% of the universe is' critique; useful when assessing credibility attacks on institutional science.
“Massive particles that do not interact with light. Therefore, they are dark and matter.” ▶ 38:04
Keating concedes dark energy is essentially a placeholder — 'a proxy for our ignorance' about what drives accelerated expansion — while insisting the accelerating expansion itself is indisputable.
thesis🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highdark energycosmology
So what: A model of how to separate a robust observation from an unexplained mechanism — the same split investors should make between a trend and its stated cause.
“we don't know what 95% of the universe is made of” ▶ 31:52
Big-science capex, funding cuts and competitive position 9
The Simons Observatory is a 100-plus-million-dollar project conceived with David Spergel and funded by Jim Simons in 2016, now operating at roughly 17,000–18,000 feet (5,200m) on a Chilean mining-field site requiring oxygen masks and hard hats.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highSimons ObservatoryJim SimonsDavid SpergelChile
So what: Concrete scale and cost benchmark for frontier cosmology infrastructure and the private philanthropy that funds it.
Simons Observatory's principal competitor — a South Pole-based experiment — was cancelled first under the Biden administration and finally killed under the Trump administration, leaving Simons as effectively the only funded, operational experiment of its kind.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highSimons ObservatorySouth PoleUS government
So what: A rare case of a research monopoly created by government defunding — concentrates both the scientific upside and the single-point-of-failure risk in one instrument.
“that was canceled by the government recently first with the Biden administration” ▶ 40:09
A potential Japanese space-based competitor called LiteBIRD could eventually exceed Simons Observatory's capability, but is not yet operational.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: mediumLiteBIRDJapanSimons Observatory
So what: The only credible successor programme in this niche is non-US and space-based — a marker for where national science leadership is migrating.
“there will be a spacebased experiment potentially from Japan called Lightbird” ▶ 40:17
A large share of the new Simons Observatory instrumentation is led out of the UK — Manchester and Queen Mary among others — enabling capabilities the US component alone could not deliver.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highUKUniversity of ManchesterQueen Mary UniversitySimons Observatory
So what: UK academic physics is a load-bearing supplier in flagship US-led science despite domestic funding pressure.
Keating states that science is undergoing significant funding cutbacks in both the US and the UK, and that support is not evenly distributed across programmes.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highUSUKscience funding
So what: Fewer, larger, more concentrated experiments — a structural shift that favours privately-funded and internationally-shared instruments.
“distributed and science is having a lot of cutbacks. Your country is doing the same.” ▶ 39:08
The project employs about 450 scientists, generates roughly a terabyte of high-quality data per day (petabytes per year), and now runs the first solar-panel array ever to power a cosmology instrument.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highSimons Observatorysolar powerdata infrastructure
So what: Frontier science is now a data-and-power problem as much as an optics problem — same bottlenecks as AI infrastructure.
“we have 450 of the smartest scientists on Earth working on this problem now” ▶ 40:39
The experiment took roughly 10 years from start to first data (received about 18–24 months ago) and will require another 8–9 years of analysis — an extraordinarily long capital-to-result cycle.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highSimons Observatory
So what: Quantifies the ~20-year duration cycle of fundamental-science bets; incompatible with most funding horizons, which is why philanthropic capital dominates.
“first data like a year and a half, two years ago and we're going to be analyzing it for the next 8 n years” ▶ 1:01:51
Keating's earlier BICEP experiment claimed detection of exactly the signal he is now hunting and had to retract it — a precedent he raises unprompted about his own current programme.
anecdote🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highBICEPSimons Observatory
So what: Discount any forthcoming detection claim from this field accordingly; the base rate of retraction in primordial gravitational-wave searches is non-zero and recent.
Keating argues academia's incentive structure caps ambition — he calls professorship 'the hardest three-hour a week job in the world' and says tenure effectively ends most researchers' ambitions.
causal🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highacademiatenure
So what: Explains why breakthrough-seeking talent increasingly exits universities for privately-funded labs — a talent-flow signal for research-heavy sectors.
“once you get tenure, you know, that's basically the end of your ambitions for many people” ▶ 51:00
Why LLMs on GPUs won't reach superintelligence 11
Keating states outright that he does not think the current LLM-plus-GPU approach — the OpenAI/Nvidia stack — will reach AGI or superintelligence, citing fundamental no-go barriers rather than scaling limits.
contrarian🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highOpenAINvidiaLLMAGI
So what: A domain-expert bear case on the core assumption underpinning AI infrastructure valuations; watch for architecture-diversification bets away from pure GPU/transformer stacks.
“I think that there's a fundamental no-go challenges that won't allow us to supersede that.” ▶ 1:16:41
Keating's mechanistic argument is that GPUs were optimised for fast matrix multiplication in video games, and being first-optimised for a task does not make an architecture the best substrate for intelligence.
causal🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highNvidiaGPULLMlinear algebra
So what: If accepted, today's hardware moat is a path-dependency artefact rather than a durable advantage — the key bear thesis on Nvidia's terminal value.
Keating reports an experiment in which an LLM was 'lobotomised' to know nothing after 1900, given identical compute and parameters, and asked to derive general relativity, quantum mechanics and the CMB — and it could not reproduce curved spacetime or Riemannian manifolds.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highLLMAlbert Einsteingeneral relativity
So what: The cleanest available empirical test of whether LLMs can generate genuinely novel science rather than recombine training data — and the reported answer is no.
“We took an LLM, we lobomized it. We said, "You don't know anything after 1900."” ▶ 1:21:11
Keating argues Einstein's equivalence-principle insight came from an embodied bodily sensation of zero gravity, and that a non-embodied system has no analogue — you cannot make an Nvidia chip feel the drop.
thesis🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highAlbert EinsteinNvidiaembodimentAI
So what: Puts embodiment — robotics, sensor-rich systems — rather than parameter count at the centre of any real AGI path.
Keating frames the core limitation as one of ontology: computers know what humans have already done, not what it means to be human — chess and the Rubik's cube were solved by machines but invented by humans.
framework🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highAIchessDeep BlueAlphaGo
So what: A test to apply to any AI product claim: is it solving a defined problem, or generating the problem definition itself?
“The computer doesn't know what it means to be human. It only knows what humans have already done.” ▶ 0:34
Keating argues LLMs cannot bootstrap on their own output — training on self-generated data is analogous to the UK's mad cow disease from cattle eating cattle — so progress remains gated on new human-created data.
causal🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highLLMsynthetic datatraining data
So what: If true, the data bottleneck is structural, and human content rights/licensing become the scarce input rather than compute.
“LLMs can't train themselves. They need human data to train it.” ▶ 1:20:21
Keating invokes Jevons' paradox to argue the AI intelligence curve is saturating: as cost per unit of intelligence falls, usage rises, but intelligence-per-dollar and intelligence-per-joule gains are flattening.
framework🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: mediumJevons paradoxAIcompute economics
So what: A saturating capability curve alongside rising usage is exactly the setup for margin compression in AI services — watch intelligence-per-dollar as the metric.
“kind of saturating the curve. This is this thing called Jeban's paradox.” ▶ 1:19:38
Keating concedes general intelligence — matching anything a human can do at a computer terminal — is achievable, but separates that sharply from superintelligence involving unpredictable, uncontrollable, unlimited capability.
framework🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: mediumAGIsuperintelligenceAlan Turing
So what: A useful two-tier definition: knowledge-work automation is investable and near; recursive superintelligence is a separate, unevidenced claim.
“But super intelligence, they can do things that we cannot predict, control, modify, and they unlimited knowledge.” ▶ 1:27:41
Keating maps Turing's halting problem onto modern AI: because you cannot in general predict whether a program halts, you cannot predict or control these systems — meaning uncontrollable AIs have already been built.
framework🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: mediumAlan Turinghalting problemAI safety
So what: A theoretical rather than empirical basis for uncontrollability — relevant to how AI regulation will be argued, and why control claims from labs are weak.
“So, we basically made all these uncontrollable AIs.” ▶ 1:26:06
Keating pushes back on extinction-risk arguments from Roman Yampolskiy and Nate Soares, noting that unpredictability alone is not evidence of catastrophe — he cannot predict his teenagers either — and sees nothing in his own physics domain that AI threatens.
contrarian🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: mediumRoman YampolskiyNate SoaresAI safety
So what: A working scientist's rejection of the doom framing; relevant to how much regulatory tail-risk to price into AI equities.
Keating criticises Sam Altman's argument that AI energy costs shouldn't be questioned because educating an 18-year-old costs more energy, calling it completely backwards because AI is a tool that must serve humanity, not a peer.
contrarian🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highSam AltmanOpenAIAI energydata centers
So what: Energy-cost framing is becoming a reputational and political flashpoint for AI labs — a regulatory and siting risk for data-center buildout.
“AI is a tool. It is not a human. Its job is to serve humanity.” ▶ 0:32
AI bubble, dependency and valuation risk 7
Keating flags OpenAI as reportedly losing around $10 billion a month and openly questions whether such economics are sustainable.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: mediumOpenAI
So what: A widely-circulating burn figure now being used by non-finance commentators as the core sustainability argument against AI-lab valuations.
“OpenAI is losing $10 billion a month or something like that” ▶ 1:28:31
Keating cites a recent OpenAI employee secondary share sale being marketed as a chance to get in at an $800 billion valuation, and questions how a loss-making company doubles from there.
contrarian🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: mediumOpenAIsecondary marketprivate valuations
So what: Secondary-market marks at $800B with an unproven path to profitability are the specific valuation datapoint to stress-test.
“this is great opportunity to get in at this $800 billion valuation I'm like how is this thing going to double from here” ▶ 1:29:00
Keating cites SpaceX's recent stock offering nearly doubling then falling to roughly half that level as evidence that private-market valuations in frontier tech are untethered from any knowable true value.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: mediumSpaceXprivate markets
So what: A live example of volatility in the marquee private-tech comparable; relevant to anyone marking illiquid frontier-tech positions.
“SpaceX, you know, had the stock offering a month ago, went up almost doubled. Now it's like way below.” ▶ 1:28:38
Keating predicts an AI bubble analogous to the 1999–2000 dot-com bubble, in which the strongest firms survive but survivorship is not evidence they were the best outcome for humanity — his Facebook/Instagram analogy.
prediction🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: mediumAI bubbledot-com bubbleFacebookInstagram
So what: The consolidation-after-crash pattern is the base case he is pricing; survivors take share but the sector's aggregate capital is impaired.
“There could be an AI bubble after that.” ▶ 1:29:38
Keating's primary AI fear is not paperclip-style extinction but economic: that humanity becomes fully dependent on AI for health, relationships and business, then is crippled when valuations collapse and the services withdraw.
thesis🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highAIOpenAIsystemic risk
So what: Reframes AI risk as a funding-continuity and dependency risk — an argument for pricing service-withdrawal scenarios into any AI-dependent business model.
“we've become completely dependent upon it. Therefore, we'll be crippled by its uh absence” ▶ 1:29:29
Keating observes that Anthropic's Claude Science product ships tooling exclusively for biology, biochemistry and pharmacology — PubMed, AlphaFold, pharma databases — with zero tools for cosmology, astrophysics or exoplanets.
data_point🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highAnthropicClaudeAlphaFoldPubMedlife sciences
So what: AI-for-science commercial effort is concentrating in life sciences where the revenue is; physical-sciences AI tooling is a visible, unserved gap.
“There was not one tool optimized for cosmology, for astrophysics, for exoplanets, for alien disclosure, nothing. It was all biology” ▶ 1:31:00
Keating became less worried about AI bioweapon misuse after speaking with Annie Jacobsen, who found the biology capabilities are heavily sandboxed and nerfed — though Jacobsen separately found a chatbot surfacing a city's vaccine stockpile location.
anecdote🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: mediumAnnie JacobsenSam AltmanAI safetybiosecurity
So what: Suggests bio-misuse guardrails are working better than headlines imply, while the residual risk is inadvertent disclosure of sensitive infrastructure data.
“basically said like all these things are completely sandboxed and nerfed when it comes to biology” ▶ 1:30:26
Trust collapse: Fauci, UAP disclosure, private epistemologies 7
Keating argues that when Fauci was angling for awards, soliciting nominations and smearing colleagues like Jay Bhattacharya, he had removed himself from the definition of scientist — a scientist never says trust me, follow me, believe in me.
thesis🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highAnthony FauciJay Bhattacharyapublic health
So what: A crisp definitional test for institutional credibility that is now being applied retroactively across public-health and regulatory bodies.
“Scientist doesn't say things like uh trust me, follow me, believe in me. There's no belief. That's religion.” ▶ 1:08:52
Keating names the specific mechanism of trust destruction: overclaiming certainty — Biden's 2020 claim that vaccination meant you wouldn't get or spread the virus — which then undermines public faith in science and government.
causal🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highJoe BidenCOVID-19vaccinespublic trust
So what: Overclaimed certainty as the causal driver of institutional distrust — the same failure mode now available to AI labs making capability claims.
“that then undermines public faith in science” ▶ 1:09:49
Keating identifies the deepest problem as everyone now having their own epistemology — personal rules for what counts as truth, such as disbelieving anything a named person or the government says — which he calls very dangerous.
thesis🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highepistemologypublic trustmedia
So what: Fragmented epistemology means no shared evidence base can resolve disputes — a structural headwind for anything requiring public consent, from vaccines to data centers.
“but they have their own epistemology which is very dangerous” ▶ 1:10:34
Keating publicly challenges Congresswoman Anna Paulina Luna — who chairs the relevant committee, has whistleblower immunity and claims interdimensional beings exist — to simply produce the body or the biologic, noting nothing prevents her.
contrarian🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highAnna Paulina LunaUAPUS CongressDavid Grusch
So what: The 'who has both the knowledge and the immunity, and still isn't producing evidence' test is the sharpest available filter on the disclosure narrative.
“I mean have you notice any bodies show up on the White House lawn?” ▶ 1:07:03
Keating notes that even hardcore UFO maximalists concede 95% of sightings have ordinary explanations, and argues the residual unexplained 5% is expected noise — as with unexplained particle tracks at the LHC — not evidence of the fantastic.
framework🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highUAPLarge Hadron Collider
So what: A general anti-Sherlock-Holmes heuristic: residual anomalies exist in every dataset and are not license to jump to the extraordinary explanation.
“95% of what we're seeing has some ordinary explanation” ▶ 1:04:38
Keating characterises the UAP field as a 'SCI op' — a scientific operation driven by a misbalanced incentive structure, replete with charlatans, grifters and frauds on both the UAP and government sides.
thesis🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highUAPUS governmentdisclosure
So what: Incentive-structure analysis rather than conspiracy explains persistent non-disclosure — a template for reading other information-scarce narratives.
“a really misbalanced incentive structure that's causing what I call a SCOP SCI operate, not PSY, but but scientific operation” ▶ 1:02:19
Keating diagnoses the structural condition sustaining both UAP and AI hype as a low-information environment in which minds fill in blanks, usually negatively — with demand for understanding far outstripping actual supply of evidence.
framework🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highUAPAIinformation markets
So what: Where evidence supply is structurally slow (decade-long experiments) and demand is instant, narrative markets — and mispricing — flourish.
“This is why it's a low information environment. People minds fill in the blanks” ▶ 1:07:28
Attention scarcity, meaning and the innocence thesis 5
Keating explicitly rejects the popular productivity claim that time is the only non-renewable resource, arguing instead that humans have abundant time and the actually scarce resource is attention.
contrarian🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highRyan Holidayattention economy
So what: Reframes the entire attention-economy investment thesis: the constraint being monetised is attention, not time — and it is being depleted by scrolling and multitasking.
“It's not time that's limited. It's attention.” ▶ 0:42
Keating's decision framework for what to work on is the intersection of four things: what is within his control, what he has capacity for, what gives him life force or energy, and what he has agency over.
framework🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highdecision-making
So what: A portable allocation heuristic for choosing between projects when the constraint is attention rather than capital.
“It's the nexus of things within my control” ▶ 55:56
Keating applies a strict uniqueness filter to his own output — he only writes books nobody else on Earth could write, and only pursues work where he cannot be scooped.
framework🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highcreative workpublishing
So what: A defensibility test — build only where you have a non-replicable position — that becomes more important as AI commoditises replicable knowledge work.
“Again, I only want to write books that only I can write.” ▶ 59:07
Keating identifies human mortality-awareness — being the only creature that knows it will die — as the source of both the urgency behind all human achievement and Ernest Becker's denial-of-death framing of civilization.
framework🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highErnest BeckerViktor Frankl
So what: The motivational asymmetry between humans and machines that underpins his argument that AI cannot originate novel science.
“We're the only creatures that know we're going to die.” ▶ 0:15
Keating warns that much of what physics publicly sells — extra dimensions, curled-up geometries, intelligent aliens — rests on a relatively weak foundation, and he sees his job as exposing where claimed understanding exceeds actual understanding.
contrarian🗣 Brian Keatingconviction: highstring theoryphysicsBrian GreeneEric Weinstein
So what: The same 'look for where the claim exceeds the evidence' discipline he applies to AGI claims; a transferable due-diligence posture.
“It's it's pretty much founded on a on a on a foundation that's relatively weak and I'm just hoping the public doesn't find out about it.” ▶ 3:33

Top Traders Unplugged 1 episode(s) · 51 insights

Most Economists Get Recessions Wrong feat. Tyler Goodspeed | Ideas Lab | Ep.5162 min51 insights2026-07-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Tyler Goodspeed (Exxon Mobil chief economist, former CEA chair) presents his book 'Recession', built on 132 US and UK recessions back to 1700: expansions never die of old age, the height/speed/duration of a boom carries zero information about the following bust, recessions do not cleanse or reallocate, and monetary/fiscal regime changes show no statistical break in recession depth or duration. He argues recessions come from sector-specific shocks with high linkages and poor near-term substitutes — energy above all — and flags rare-earth export restrictions from China as the shock type most analogous to historical energy, steel and cotton disruptions.
Expansions never die of old age · 6Plucking beats oscillation · 6Policy regimes barely move the cycle · 6Credit booms and financial-crisis recoveries · 4Energy and sector-specific shocks as the real cause · 6Banking structure as shock amplifier: US vs UK · 4Post-2008 bank regulation as the UK's missing recovery · 5Recessions do not cleanse or reallocate · 5Randomness, base rates and the 2026 risk map · 9
Expansions never die of old age 6
Using 132 US and UK recessions extended back to 1700, Goodspeed finds an economic expansion is statistically no more likely to end in its 10th year than in its first or second year, on either side of the Atlantic and both before and after 1945.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highUnited StatesUnited KingdomNBERTyler Goodspeed
So what: Positioning that de-risks purely because a cycle is 'late' has no historical base-rate support; expansion age is not a valid recession signal.
“economic expansions have never died of old age on either side of the Atlantic, either before or after 1945” ▶ 9:05
Recessions, unlike expansions, DO die of old age: the single strongest predictor of whether a recession ends in the next quarter or year is simply how old that recession already is, true both pre- and post-1945.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highUnited StatesUnited Kingdom
So what: Duration-since-onset is a usable timing input for re-risking inside a downturn, in a way that expansion age never is for de-risking.
“statistically recessions do die of old age and always have. And in fact the single strongest predictor of whether a recession is going to end in the next quarter or the next year is simply how old the recession is” ▶ 11:42
Goodspeed frames the debate as Peter Pan (expansions never age but can be killed by an external shock) versus the picture of Dorian Gray (hidden excesses accumulate until a reckoning), and concludes the data support Peter Pan.
framework🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highPeter PanDorian Gray
So what: A reusable mental model for rejecting 'accumulated excess' narratives and refocusing on identifying the shock, not the vintage of the cycle.
The standard post-1945 evidence base of only 12 US recessions is statistically inadequate — quarterly or monthly frequency creates the illusion of degrees of freedom that the 12 cycle observations do not actually provide.
framework🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highNBERUnited States
So what: Discount macro research and recession models calibrated only to the postwar US sample; they are fitting a dozen events.
“you think that with quarterly or monthly data you have all these statistical degrees of freedom, but you really only have 12” ▶ 5:00
Goodspeed rebuilt the recession chronology using NBER quarterly data back to 1854, annual data to 1790, the Davis and Romer alternative chronologies (because NBER overstates the frequency and duration of pre-1927 US recessions), and colonial American statistics back to 1700.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highNBERChristina RomerDavisDiebold and Rudebusch
So what: Earlier findings that pre-1945 expansions died of old age (Diebold and Rudebusch) reverse once the chronology is corrected — the raw NBER series biases long-history cycle work.
Goodspeed began the project believing the 2020 pandemic recession was uniquely exogenous, with no excess or misallocation needing remedy, and concluded from the longer sample that essentially every recession fits that description.
thesis🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: high2020 pandemic recession2008 financial crisis
So what: Reframes 2020 from an outlier to the template: exogenous shocks, not endogenous excess, are the normal recession mechanism.
Plucking beats oscillation 6
There is no informational content in the height, speed or duration of an economic expansion about the depth, speed, duration or even probability of the recession that follows.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highGDPconsumptioninvestmentbank lending
So what: Boom-magnitude indicators (credit growth, investment surges, skyscraper records) should be dropped as recession-severity predictors.
“There there's simply not nothing no informational content in the height, speed, or duration of an economic expansion that can tell you anything about the depth, speed, duration, or even probability of the subsequent recession.” ▶ 14:40
The relationship runs only one way: the depth and speed of a recession does predict the height and speed of the subsequent recovery, which Goodspeed summarizes as expansions obeying Newton's third law while recessions perennially violate it.
framework🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highplucking model
So what: Deeper drawdowns imply sharper snapbacks — a basis for sizing recovery trades off recession depth rather than off prior boom size.
“economic expansions adhere to Newton's third law of motion, uh that to every action there's an equal and opposite reaction, uh whereas economic recessions are perennial violators of Newton's third law” ▶ 15:54
Economies are best modeled as growing close to potential, taking adverse shocks that push them temporarily below trend, then recovering — not as rising above potential and enduring an inevitable corrective overshoot.
framework🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highpotential GDPlong-run trend
So what: Implies output gaps are one-sided; 'overheating' narratives that justify pre-emptive tightening rest on a model the long history does not support.
The policy corollary of the plucking model is that the two things that matter are maximizing the long-run trend growth rate and not obstructing the recovery back to trend — the host's framing, which Goodspeed endorses.
thesis🗣 Kevin Coldiron / Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highfiscal policymonetary policy
So what: A policy scorecard for evaluating administrations: trend-growth measures and non-interference in rebounds, not cycle management.
Goodspeed argues we should worry at least as much about expansions as recessions, since the majority of years economies expand matters more for long-run prosperity than the minority in which they contract — citing that the UK has had far fewer recessions than the US over 200 years yet has long been about 30% poorer.
contrarian🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highUnited KingdomUnited States
So what: Fewer recessions is not the objective function; countries optimizing for cycle stability can underperform on level of income.
“the UK has had many fewer recessions over the past 200 years than the United States, but the UK is also and has long been about 30% poorer” ▶ 17:28
Goodspeed warns policymakers against 'medicating or sedating' expansions that would otherwise die healthy and innocent, since recessions will happen regardless because history will keep happening.
thesis🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highFederal Reservepolicymakers
So what: A case against pre-emptive cycle-killing tightening; the cost is trend growth and the recession still arrives from an unrelated shock.
Policy regimes barely move the cycle 6
Goodspeed's findings imply monetary policy matters less than commonly assumed, because recession depth and duration have been remarkably constant across four centuries of radically different monetary regimes.
contrarian🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: mediumFederal Reservemonetary policy
So what: Supports rule-based over discretionary policy and argues against pricing central-bank skill as a major cycle variable.
“I think it would suggest that maybe monetary policy doesn't matter quite as much as we tend to think” ▶ 18:54
Within-cycle macro volatility for GDP, consumption, investment and bank lending has declined since 1945, but only back down to levels that already prevailed before 1914 — making the 1914-1945 period the true outlier rather than the modern era an achievement.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highGDPconsumptioninvestmentbank lending
So what: Undercuts the Great Moderation as an institutional accomplishment; volatility regimes may simply mean-revert to a pre-WWI norm.
“macroeconomic volatility for GDP, for consumption, for investment, bank lending has declined since 1945, it's actually only declined to levels that prevailed before 1914” ▶ 19:29
Recessions have become less frequent in both the US and UK, but there is no statistically detectable break point at 1913 (founding of the Federal Reserve), 1946 (UK Beveridge reforms), the 1930s New Deal, or the 1960s Great Society — the trend is gradual, not institutional.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highFederal ReserveBeveridge reformsGreat SocietyUnited Kingdom
So what: Removes the standard justification for crediting central banking or the welfare state with cycle smoothing; look elsewhere for the driver.
The one possible trend break toward longer-lived US expansions Goodspeed identifies is 1785, coinciding with the United States leaving behind colonial-era armed conflict — not any monetary or fiscal institution.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: mediumUnited Statescolonial America
So what: Points to conflict/geopolitical shock frequency, rather than macro policy machinery, as the historical driver of expansion length.
“Um there's possibly one exception. And that is 1785 in the United States.” ▶ 21:30
Recession depth has been statistically no different since 1945 than before, and UK recessions have actually been LONGER since 1945 than before, contradicting the expectation that a more interventionist countercyclical state would shorten and shallow downturns.
contrarian🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highUnited Kingdomfiscal policy
So what: Automatic stabilizers and discretionary fiscal response should not be modeled as materially reducing drawdown depth in advanced economies.
“And if anything, UK recessions have actually been living longer since 1945 than before.” ▶ 36:47
Goodspeed explicitly separates the aggregate result from the normative one: aggregate recovery does not mean every household recovers, so he argues for targeted relief through unemployment insurance including higher replacement rates in high-unemployment conditions, as seen in 2020.
thesis🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highunemployment insurance2020 pandemic recession
So what: Signals where he expects future policy response to concentrate — transfers and UI rather than broad stimulus or cycle management.
“that is not necessarily an argument for inaction” ▶ 38:10
Credit booms and financial-crisis recoveries 4
The height, speed and volume of bank credit growth during US and UK expansions does not explain variation in the depth or speed of the bank credit contraction in the following recession.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highbank creditUnited StatesUnited Kingdom
So what: Credit-gap indicators widely used by macroprudential regulators lack predictive power for the size of the subsequent credit bust on average.
Goodspeed finds UK recovery speed back to 1700 is no different whether or not the recession coincided with a financial crisis, measured both with Stephen Broadberry's binary crisis indicator and with the volume of bank-credit contraction.
contrarian🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highStephen BroadberryUnited Kingdomfinancial crises
So what: Directly challenges the Reinhart-Rogoff prior that post-financial-crisis recoveries are structurally slow, a core assumption in post-crisis growth forecasts.
“the speed of of economic recoveries for the United Kingdom going all the way back to 1700 is is no different if that recession involved a financial crisis or not” ▶ 25:00
Mike Bordo of Rutgers found the same result for the United States back to the 1880s — recoveries were no slower when a recession coincided with a financial crisis.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highMike BordoRutgersUnited States
So what: Two independent long-history datasets now contradict the slow-recovery-after-crisis stylized fact for the Anglo-American economies.
Goodspeed reconciles his result with Reinhart and Rogoff's 'This Time Is Different' by arguing their broader country sample introduces correlates — the reasons those countries had crisis-coincident recessions — that are themselves correlated with slow recovery.
causal🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: mediumCarmen ReinhartKen RogoffThis Time Is Different
So what: Suggests the slow-recovery finding is an emerging-market/institutional-quality selection effect, not a mechanical property of financial crises.
Energy and sector-specific shocks as the real cause 6
Oil shocks were major contributors to US recessions in 1948, 1953, 1957, 1970, 1973, 1980, 1981, 1990, and — underappreciated — 2001 and 2008.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highoilUnited Statesrecessions
So what: Energy supply disruption is the single most repeated recession trigger in the postwar US record, including in two downturns usually attributed to other causes.
“They were major contributors to US recessions in 1948, '53, '57, '70, '73, '80, '81, '90, and underappreciated 2001 and 2008” ▶ 27:46
Goodspeed distinguishes broad macro shocks that hit all sectors roughly equally (e.g. a pandemic) from sector-specific shocks that hit one or two highly linked sectors whose output is hard to substitute within 12 months — and finds the sector-specific type has been more important historically.
framework🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highenergycoalcottonpandemic
So what: A screening framework: monitor chokepoint sectors with high input linkages and low short-run substitutability, not just aggregate macro data.
“are sector-specific shocks that maybe only directly impact one or two sectors, but those sectors have very high linkages to the rest of the economy” ▶ 29:15
Before oil, industrial action in the coal industry was a perennial recession contributor on both sides of the Atlantic, and further back adverse harvest shocks acted as energy shocks because animal feed (provender) fueled draft-animal transport, while bad UK winters destroyed peat supply used for residential heating and industrial power.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highcoalpeatUnited Kingdomharvest shocks
So what: Establishes energy-as-recession-trigger as a 300-year invariant rather than a 1970s artifact, raising the prior on future energy-driven downturns.
The highest-ever price of a barrel of oil or gallon of gas was not the 1973 Arab oil embargo or the 1990 Iraqi invasion of Kuwait but June 2008, and overall US energy prices have never been higher since 1945 than in summer 2008.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highoilJune 2008Arab oil embargoKuwait
So what: Reframes the GFC as at least partly an energy shock, which changes what indicators to watch for a repeat.
“the highest ever price of a barrel of oil or a gallon of gas uh was not during the Arab oil embargo of 1973 or the Iraqi invasion Kuwait in 1990. It was in June 2008.” ▶ 49:01
By summer 2008 the average American household was spending about $2,000 more per year on energy goods and services than a couple of years earlier, against a backdrop where roughly half of US households have no after-tax savings and energy uses (commuting, school runs, heating, food) are hard to economize on.
causal🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highUS householdsenergy prices
So what: Provides a transmission channel — household energy budget squeeze — for translating oil spikes into consumer credit stress.
“by summer 2008, the average American household was having to spend $2,000 more per year on energy goods and services” ▶ 49:31
Goodspeed links that energy squeeze directly to the mortgage crisis: by summer 2008 about 5% of American homeowners were seriously delinquent on their mortgage.
causal🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highmortgagesUS homeowners2008 financial crisis
So what: Implies mortgage delinquency waves can be triggered by cost-of-living shocks, not only by underwriting quality — a different early-warning set.
“by summer 2008, about 5% of American homeowners were seriously delinquent on their mortgage” ▶ 50:06
Banking structure as shock amplifier: US vs UK 4
The UK's historically lower recession frequency is explained not by temperament but by structure: from 1826 the UK had a nationwide branch banking system, so weakness in one region or sector could be offset by strength elsewhere, like a diversified portfolio.
causal🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highUnited Kingdombranch banking1826
So what: Banking-system diversification is a first-order determinant of national recession vulnerability — relevant to comparing today's banking structures across countries.
“since 1826 in the United Kingdom you had a nationwide system of branch banking” ▶ 31:24
For most of US history until the 1980s, banks could not operate across state lines or even more than one branch within a state, leaving tens of thousands of tiny, undercapitalized, under-diversified institutions that amplified and propagated adverse shocks.
causal🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highUnited Statesregional banksunit banking
So what: US recession-proneness was partly a regulatory artifact; the same lens applies to today's regional bank concentration in specific sectors and regions.
“not only could you not operate a bank across state lines you couldn't even operate more than one bank branch within a state” ▶ 31:58
The UK's second insulation was fuel mix: it was still coal-intensive when US recessions of 1948, 1953, 1957 and 1970 were oil-driven, and for idiosyncratic reasons went from 1926 to 1972 without a single official coal strike, allowing it to go from a 1947 postwar recession to no recession until 1973.
causal🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highUnited Kingdomcoaloil1973
So what: Illustrates that a country's energy composition, not its policy skill, determined which global shocks it absorbed — a template for assessing today's national exposures.
“they went from 1926 to 1972 without a single official coal strike” ▶ 32:55
Goodspeed and the host frame national economies as portfolios with different sector compositions, meaning the analyst's job is less about forecasting recession timing and more about identifying which shock types a given economy is exposed to versus insulated against.
framework🗣 Kevin Coldiron / Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highsector compositionUnited StatesUnited Kingdom
So what: Reorients macro research toward exposure mapping — a tractable exercise — instead of recession-date forecasting, which the book shows is impossible.
Post-2008 bank regulation as the UK's missing recovery 5
The UK's failure to return to its pre-2008 growth trend — both a level effect and a rate-of-change effect — is one of the very few exceptions to the plucking pattern across three and a half centuries.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highUnited Kingdom2008 financial crisis
So what: Marks the UK post-2008 experience as a genuine structural break worth explaining rather than a slow cyclical recovery.
Goodspeed attributes it to a post-2008 policy regime change that effectively mandated economies produce with less bank credit — liquidity coverage ratios, supplementary leverage ratios, tiered capital requirements and stress testing — inducing substitution of public-sector lending for private-sector lending.
causal🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highliquidity coverage ratiosupplemental leverage ratioscapital requirementsstress testingbank credit
So what: Frames bank capital and liquidity rules as a permanent negative supply shock to a hard-to-substitute input — a live argument in current deregulation debates.
“all of these measures that effectively induced the substitution of lending to the public sector for lending to the private sector” ▶ 45:47
The regulatory shock bit harder in the UK than the US because the UK was historically far more reliant on bank credit for corporate external financing, while the US has a robust venture capital and private equity ecosystem plus thousands of regional relationship lenders.
causal🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highventure capitalprivate equityregional banksUnited Kingdom
So what: Non-bank financing depth is a structural buffer against regulatory tightening — a durable US advantage over bank-dependent economies.
The UK has long had fewer but on average bigger banks, so a larger share of its banking system fell inside the scope of the post-2009 regime — a reminder that no policy comes without trade-offs.
causal🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highUnited Kingdombank concentration
So what: Size-threshold regulation binds differently by market structure; concentrated banking systems absorb more of any given rule change.
“the United Kingdom has and has long had fewer banks that are on average bigger. And so they were all more likely to be treated by the post-2009 policy regime.” ▶ 46:35
Goodspeed accepts the host's more cynical reading that post-crisis rules pushing banks into sovereign debt resembled financial repression, noting it looks much like how the UK and US worked down their post-WWII debt burdens.
thesis🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: mediumfinancial repressionsovereign debtUnited KingdomUnited States
So what: If today's deficits require captive bank demand, expect regulatory settings that keep banks buying govvies — a persistent bid for sovereign paper and a drag on private credit.
“whether it was deliberate or subconscious, I mean it did effectively resemble financial repression” ▶ 47:53
Recessions do not cleanse or reallocate 5
Goodspeed finds that creative destruction — the movement of people and capital from less to more efficient firms — is impaired rather than enhanced by recessions, despite his own prior wish that recessions served some purpose.
contrarian🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highcreative destructionproductivity
So what: Removes the intellectual case for tolerating downturns as purgative; downturns destroy rather than improve the allocation of resources.
“I find that that process is impaired rather than enhanced by recessions” ▶ 39:53
Research and development spending is procyclical — it rises during expansions and falls during recessions — so downturns reduce rather than reallocate innovation investment.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highR&Dinnovation
So what: Recessions carry a durable hit to the innovation pipeline, arguing against models where downturns raise future productivity growth.
“research and development is procyclical. So, it rises more during economic expansions and then falls during economic recessions” ▶ 40:08
The productivity ladder — workers quitting for better-matched, more productive jobs — collapses in recessions because quits plummet as no one dares leave a job and hiring plummets as firms don't dare take on workers, leaving a period of poor employer-employee matches during and after the downturn.
causal🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highlabor marketquits ratehiring
So what: Quits rate is the mechanism to watch for productivity reallocation; its collapse implies a lingering productivity cost beyond the recession itself.
“that whole ladder, that whole productivity ladder, just collapses during economic recessions because quitting plummets, because no one dares to leave a job, and hiring plummets because firms don't dare to take on new workers” ▶ 40:45
Recessions are 'rampant age discriminators' against younger workers and younger, often more dynamic firms — firms that lack physical collateral to pledge and lack a long credit history for bank loans.
causal🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highyoung firmsyoung workersbank lendingcollateral
So what: Recessions entrench incumbents, implying downturns can be a stagnation mechanism rather than a renewal mechanism — relevant to competitive-moat and small-cap analysis.
“recessions are rampant age discriminators. They discriminate against younger workers. They discriminate against younger, often more dynamic firms” ▶ 40:15
Testing reallocation directly, Goodspeed finds the allocation of people and output across sectors at the end of a recovery looks very similar to how it would have looked had the economy continued uninterrupted along trend.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highsector allocationemploymentoutput
So what: Investors should not expect recessions to accelerate structural rotation between sectors; post-recession sector mix reverts to counterfactual trend.
“the allocation, looks pretty darn similar to how it would have looked had it continued uninterrupted along trend” ▶ 41:35
Randomness, base rates and the 2026 risk map 9
Goodspeed cites Soviet economist Eugen Slutsky, who built a moving-sum series from old Imperial Russian lottery ticket numbers that mapped almost perfectly onto an index of British business conditions over nearly half a century — showing random causes summed together can look cyclical.
framework🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highEugen Slutskybusiness cycleBritish business conditions
So what: Cycle-shaped data is not evidence of a cycle; a warning against cycle-timing models built on apparent periodicity.
“It was actually a Soviet economist in Moscow, Eugen Slutsky, who took a bunch of old Imperial Russian lottery tickets and created a moving sum series of the numbers in the lottery tickets.” ▶ 51:54
Goodspeed states plainly that his book demonstrates recessions are unforecastable and unpredictable, but argues the useful response is base-rate updating rather than resignation.
thesis🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highrecession forecasting
So what: Reframes macro process from point forecasts to conditional probability adjustment around historical unconditional frequencies.
“recessions are unforecastable and unpredictable” ▶ 52:40
Goodspeed offers explicit unconditional annual base rates: roughly a 1-in-100 chance of a pandemic-related recession, about 1-in-10 of an energy-related recession in the US (with that probability declining), and about 1-in-50 of a credit-controls-related recession.
data_point🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highpandemicenergycredit controls
So what: Directly usable priors for scenario weighting — and the declining energy number implies less recession risk per barrel of oil shock than history suggests.
“In any given year in the United States, there's been about a one in ten uh chance of an energy-related recession” ▶ 53:15
The optimistic finding is that households, businesses and investors have been getting steadily better at absorbing recession-generating shocks since at least 1700, driven mainly by diversification of banking systems and of energy systems both across and within fuels.
thesis🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highdiversificationbanking systemsenergy systems
So what: Structural shock absorption is improving, arguing for a lower unconditional recession probability going forward than long-history averages imply.
“our banking systems generally are are more diversified um than they were previously, in earlier periods. And our energy systems are more diversified both across fuels and and within fuels.” ▶ 54:15
Goodspeed was concerned at the start of the Iran conflict because physical barrels and cubic feet were not flowing into global markets, and said that had the conflict persisted at March-April intensity he finds it hard to see the US or global economy avoiding recession.
prediction🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: mediumIranoilglobal economy
So what: Establishes a concrete conditional: sustained physical supply loss at that intensity was, in his view, a recession-grade shock.
“if the conflict were to have persisted at the intensity of of say March, April, it it's hard for me to to to see uh the global economy or the US economy not having incurred recession” ▶ 56:47
Four differences make 2026 less oil-fragile than 1973: a dollar of output is far less energy- and oil-intensive, non-core OPEC supply is both a bigger share of the market and more elastic, the energy stack is more diverse, and advanced economies now hold strategic petroleum reserves as a hedge.
framework🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highOPECstrategic petroleum reservesoil intensity1973
So what: Explains observed market resilience to the Iran shock and argues for smaller macro betas to oil spikes than 1970s analogies imply.
“a dollar of economic output is today uh much less energy-intensive generally and oil-intensive uh specifically than it was in 1973” ▶ 57:06
The shock Goodspeed thinks is underweighted by others is the threatened Chinese export restrictions on rare-earth elements, currently deferred until the autumn, which he compares to historical energy, steel and cotton supply disruptions with broad spillover effects.
prediction🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: mediumrare earth elementsPeople's Republic of Chinaexport restrictionssteelcotton
So what: A named, dated catalyst: rare-earth supply restriction fits his high-linkage, low-substitutability recession template better than macro-level worries.
“that's currently deferred until the autumn” ▶ 58:43
Goodspeed argues morally laden crisis narratives with villains, heroes and resolution set in very early — 1772, 1857, 1873, 1929, 2008 — with Bernanke, Paulson and Geithner describing themselves as firefighters, while the actual causes are usually more prosaic.
framework🗣 Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highBen BernankeHank PaulsonTim Geithner2008 financial crisis
So what: A bias check: the dominant story of any crisis forms fast and is usually wrong, so build positions from mechanism rather than narrative.
The economy is described as remarkably robust — a single shock is generally not enough, and it typically takes multiple shocks of different natures hitting around the same time to produce a recession.
framework🗣 Kevin Coldiron / Tyler Goodspeedconviction: highrecessionshocks
So what: Sets a high bar for recession calls: look for shock coincidence across unrelated channels, not a single alarming data series.

a16z Podcast 1 episode(s) · 50 insights

How Lassie Is Automating Healthcare Administration59 min50 insights2026-07-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Lassie's founders (Stein, ex-Robin Hood; Frederick, ex-Superhuman) and an a16z partner (Alex) argue that AI's real TAM is not software budgets but labor budgets: Lassie sells a fully autonomous billing/payments agent to US dental practices where 160,000 offices each spend ~$200k/year on admin and literally cannot hire staff. The conversation details how they bootstrapped automation by doing the billing work by hand in offices, why 98% autonomy with no human in the loop is the product requirement, why verticals with no incumbent software vendor are structurally safer than 'Workday but AI', and how a federal mandate forcing insurers to offer direct deposit (70% of these businesses are still paid on paper) is the regulatory unlock that makes the work automatable at all.
Software doing labor, not storing data · 7The Betty shortage: SMB admin as the wedge · 7Building agents with no human in the loop · 7Moats when the category has no software incumbent · 6Reaching Main Street: SMB go-to-market and onboarding · 6Paper-to-digital: the regulatory inflection · 5Insurance dynamics and the marginal cost of arguing · 4Master plan, model limits and building a 2026 company · 8
Software doing labor, not storing data 7
Alex frames the entire history of software as digitizing filing cabinets — Sabre for airline reservations, PeopleSoft for HR, LexisNexis for legal, QuickBooks and NetSuite for accounting — which moved storage but left humans doing the actual work.
framework🗣 Alexconviction: highSabre SystemsIBMAmerican AirlinesPeopleSoftLexisNexisQuickBooksNetSuiteWorkday
So what: If storage-era SaaS never removed headcount, the incremental value of AI that actually executes the work is a step-change, not a multiple of existing software spend.
“software just kind of took things that were stored in paper format and then they made them available first on prem via green screen computers but people still had to do the work” ▶ 0:03
Alex claims software historically did not make organizations more efficient: HR departments employed roughly the same number of people per company size in 1950 as in 2000, with filing-cabinet guards simply replaced by IT departments and a CISO.
contrarian🗣 Alexconviction: mediumPeopleSoftWorkdayHR softwareCISO
So what: A deflationary read on legacy SaaS ROI; supports rotating exposure from workflow tools toward vendors billing against labor line items.
“the same number of people worked in HR for the exact same size company in like 1950 as probably 2000” ▶ 8:56
Alex's core thesis is that the volume of work performed on stored information is orders of magnitude larger than the market for storing that information, so AI that edits the filing cabinet rather than just holding it addresses a vastly bigger market.
thesis🗣 Alexconviction: highAI agentssoftware market
So what: Sizes the AI opportunity off services and payroll spend rather than the ~$1T software market, justifying far higher revenue ceilings per vertical.
“So it just turns out that the work is orders of magnitude bigger than the storage of information that the work is done on.” ▶ 9:54
Alex uses Toast to argue fintech bundling was the prior TAM expansion: restaurant software was technically possible on MS-DOS in 1985 but no restaurant would pay $100,000 for it, whereas a ~2% payments take rate lets you collect that amount invisibly.
framework🗣 Alexconviction: highToastIBM PCMicrosoft DOSrestaurantspayments
So what: The same monetization logic now applies to labor: charging out of the payroll budget is the next bundling wave after payments attach.
“nobody would pay $100,000 for that. But if you bundle in payment processing, you're effectively charging $100,000 for that, right? Because maybe you get a 2% vig.” ▶ 11:31
Alex argues selling labor beats selling software because the vendor can charge for work done cheaper and better than humans — but says even that framing undersells it, since in many cases no human is available at any price.
thesis🗣 Alexconviction: highAI agentslabor marketsmall businesses
So what: Demand for AI labor in SMB services is not a cost-substitution trade; it's filling unfillable roles, which supports pricing power and low churn.
“It's not like, oh, AI is going to take the jobs. In many cases, you can't find somebody.” ▶ 0:53
Alex models unmet SMB demand as a market failure — everything to the right of the supply-demand equilibrium — where a service everyone would pay a dollar for costs $100 to produce, and only near-zero-cost AI labor clears it.
framework🗣 Alexconviction: highlabor marketAI agents
So what: A screening heuristic: hunt for roles that are economically rational to want but irrational to hire, since those convert instantly to AI seats.
“imagine that there is something that every human on earth would pay a dollar for but the cost of manufacturing that thing is $100. You just have a market failure” ▶ 13:37
Alex says the public markets are pricing software as dead and AI as the winner, while VCs treat AI as software — and that pure-play software companies not shipping AI features are 'smoking crack' because customers are demanding them.
contrarian🗣 Alexconviction: highpublic marketsWorkdayNetSuitesoftware stocksAI
So what: Flags a market dislocation: the software/AI dichotomy in public multiples may be false, creating both value traps and mispriced incumbents.
“if you look at the public markets the public markets are saying in many cases like oh you're a software company software is dead, software socks, software is zero. Oh, you're an AI. It's the opposite of what VCs are saying.” ▶ 26:54
The Betty shortage: SMB admin as the wedge 7
Lassie was founded after Stein watched his own dentist, Dr. Quan, the number-one-rated doctor on Yelp, spend 200 hours a month on paperwork and submit insurance claims by hand because he could not hire billing staff.
anecdote🗣 Steinconviction: highLassieDr. QuanYelpdental practicesRobin Hood
So what: The founding observation quantifies the labor pool being displaced: 200 hours/month per practice is the revenue pool an agent can attack.
“the number one rated doctor on Yelp spending 200 hours a month on paperwork and busy work” ▶ 2:03
Stein cites roughly 160,000 dental practices in the US each spending about $200,000 a year on administrative costs, which he converts into a ~$1 billion recurring-revenue market for dental alone.
data_point🗣 Steinconviction: highdental practicesLassieUnited States
So what: A concrete bottom-up TAM for vertical SMB AI; the same arithmetic can be applied to other practitioner verticals.
“there about like 160,000 dental practices in the US alone and like they spend roughly $200,000 a year on like administrative costs” ▶ 14:56
Lassie is already charging five figures per year for a first agent that performs only about 30 hours of labor a month against roughly 200 hours of available work, implying substantial headroom to expand contract value per practice.
data_point🗣 Steinconviction: highLassiedental practices
So what: Land-and-expand economics are explicit: ~15% of the addressable labor at a five-figure ACV suggests multi-fold net revenue retention potential.
“we are already charging five figures for kind of like this first agent that only does 30 hours uh of labor a month and there's 200 hours of labors to be done for Dr. Loop” ▶ 16:16
Alex recounts that his childhood dentist Dr. Sloop retired specifically because he lost the woman who did his books and could not replace her, and told Alex he would not have retired if Lassie had existed.
anecdote🗣 Alexconviction: highDr. SloopLassiedental practices
So what: Admin burden is causing practitioner supply to exit the market — AI admin tools may increase practitioner supply, not just cut cost.
“And he said that if this had been around, he wouldn't have retired.” ▶ 13:14
Stein says Lassie encounters doctors with Harvard degrees personally doing billing until midnight, and that these owners are close to quitting the parts of the job they hate — which makes them adopt AI fast rather than skeptically.
causal🗣 Steinconviction: highLassiedental practicesAI adoption
So what: Contradicts the assumed SMB AI-skepticism barrier; the binding constraint is reach, not persuasion.
“these people are in real pain and they are to Alex's point about to quit or just like they hate their job” ▶ 15:39
Alex characterizes the competitive incumbent in dental administration not as a software vendor but as departed staff — 'the incumbent was named Betty and she quit two weeks ago' — or an offshore billing agency.
framework🗣 Alexconviction: highdental practicesbilling agenciesLassie
So what: Competitive analysis for AI-labor companies should benchmark against wage rates and outsourcing agencies, not SaaS seat prices.
“Well, the incumbent was named Betty and she quit two weeks ago. That's the incumbent.” ▶ 27:44
Stein describes the daily grind Lassie replaces as updating roughly 200 ledgers a week, visiting insurance portals, updating a system of record and checking the bank account 200 times a week — and says cutting that to a handful saves 10 to 20 hours.
data_point🗣 Steinconviction: highdental practicesinsurance portalsLassie
So what: Quantifies the value delivered per customer, anchoring both pricing and the ROI story used in word-of-mouth sales.
“you have like to update 200 ledgers a week because that's how much patience you see and then you have to like go to an insurance portal update like a system of record check against the bank account 200 times a week” ▶ 20:40
Building agents with no human in the loop 7
Frederick decomposes job automation into three layers — context (historical data, patient records), tools to do the work, and intelligence — and says Lassie built context and tools from 2020 when models were weak, then swapped in better models as an unearned tailwind.
framework🗣 Frederickconviction: highLassiereasoning modelspatient records
So what: A durable build order for vertical AI: proprietary context and write-access tooling compound while the intelligence layer commoditizes.
“we started building uh building the context layer and building the tools and it and it's just that the intelligence layer wasn't that intelligent” ▶ 6:38
Lassie bootstrapped automation by having its founders act as the humans in the loop — literally doing billing inside offices — and then automating away their own work, which Frederick says was necessary to build a product that works.
framework🗣 Frederickconviction: highLassieDr. QuanDr. ShaScranton Pennsylvania
So what: A repeatable playbook (and a diligence question) for services-to-software AI companies: did the team do the job before encoding it?
“Initially we were actually the humans in the loop. We kind of automated away our own problems.” ▶ 0:25
Frederick sets an internal bar of roughly 95%+ automation before selling a product, deliberately refusing to wait for 100% and instead moving to the next job once one is taken over, optimizing share of the whole business automated rather than per-product perfection.
framework🗣 Frederickconviction: highLassieautomation
So what: Defines a shippable-autonomy threshold for agent products and explains why breadth beats depth in expanding ACV.
“we want to get to like a sufficient level of automation across any product to uh before we sell it. So you know for us I think that's like 95 plus say but not necessarily 100” ▶ 19:22
Stein argues SMB AI cannot use the human-in-the-loop pattern most AI companies rely on, because there is no staffer left to review output — forcing an agent that errs on the side of correctness and takes years longer to build.
causal🗣 Steinconviction: highLassieSMBsAI agents
So what: Raises the technical bar (and the moat) for SMB agents relative to enterprise copilots that can lean on reviewers.
“we needed to build an agent. That's why it took us years that like airs on the side of correctness” ▶ 16:54
Frederick says the structural difference between SMB and enterprise software is that in SMBs there is nobody available to use the tool at all, so tool-shaped products fail regardless of quality.
thesis🗣 Frederickconviction: highSMBsenterprisesLassie
So what: Explains why SMB SaaS historically underperformed and why outcome-delivery pricing is the only viable SMB AI model.
“a huge difference uh between SMBs in general and and enterprises is that in SMBs there's nobody to to use the tools” ▶ 18:24
Lassie says thousands of staffers across its customer base feed corrections back into the agent, and Stein claims about 98% of the work is handled by the first agent with a sliver left for humans.
data_point🗣 Steinconviction: mediumLassiedental practices
So what: A distributed human-feedback loop across many small customers is a data moat unavailable to single-enterprise deployments.
“it's fine if it does in this case for the first agent 98% of the work and then there's a sliver left” ▶ 21:35
Stein says the defensibility comes from stitching read-and-write integrations across systems and building an ontology so every party agrees on what an insurance claim or patient payment means, since each system defines them differently.
thesis🗣 Steinconviction: highLassiepractice management systemsinsurance claimsontology
So what: The moat in vertical AI is data-model and integration work, not model access — hard to replicate with a coding agent.
“you need to build an ontology to make sure that everybody in this whole ecosystem is on the same page about an insurance claim and the patient payment because all these different systems have like a slightly different definition of that” ▶ 28:23
Moats when the category has no software incumbent 6
Alex restates his rule that the battle between startup and incumbent comes down to whether the startup gets distribution before the incumbent gets the innovation, and warns that AI shortens the incumbent's time to innovation.
framework🗣 Alexconviction: highstartupsincumbentsAI
So what: Raises the bar for feature-shaped AI startups sitting on top of an existing platform; speed of distribution is the only defense.
“the battle between every startup and incumbent comes down to whether the startup gets the distribution before the incumbent gets the innovation” ▶ 26:08
Alex argues AI erodes a key reason incumbents fail to copy startups — that big companies hire bad engineers and carry heavy process — because AI turns a bad engineer into a decent one.
contrarian🗣 Alexconviction: mediumincumbentsAI coding toolsengineering
So what: Implies shorter windows for AI feature startups and a re-rating of incumbents' ability to defend, particularly in categories they already own.
“one of the reasons is they hire very bad engineers and they have lots of process, but now AI kind of makes a bad engineer into like a pretty good engineer, you know, kind of” ▶ 25:11
Alex's 'TiVo problem' framework says companies that build a feature on someone else's pipe face only bad outcomes: a control discount from the one buyer who can acquire you, a worse copy years later, or a licensing deal where the distributor takes the economics.
framework🗣 Alexconviction: highTiVoReplayTVComcastTime Warner Cable
So what: A diligence filter: verify a startup owns the customer relationship or pipe, not just the differentiated feature.
“you you have what what I call a control discount versus a control premium” ▶ 23:15
Alex says his TrialPay-era lesson was that startups should build the boring pipe first — he realized four years in he should have built Stripe, because owning commodity payment processing wins the customer chronologically and lets you add the lucrative layer on top.
framework🗣 Alexconviction: highStripeTrialPaypayments
So what: Supports backing 'boring infrastructure first' AI companies over feature-led ones, since ownership of the pipe gates the profitable products.
“wow we should we should do boring payment processing which is a commodity business because if we do that then we own the customer” ▶ 24:02
Alex uses X1 (Outlook search, VC-funded, eventually sold to Yahoo for little) as the cautionary case of building an obviously great feature in a category the platform owner would inevitably absorb.
anecdote🗣 Alexconviction: highX1Microsoft OutlookIdealabYahoo
So what: Historical analog for today's AI wrappers built on top of dominant productivity suites.
Alex's key AI-era amendment is that many AI-does-the-work categories are non-categories with no software incumbent at all, because the only prior solution was human labor — which removes the platform-absorption risk entirely.
thesis🗣 Alexconviction: highdental softwareWorkdayAI agentsSMBs
So what: The cleanest screen for AI investing: prefer verticals where labor, not software, is the incumbent; be wary of 'Workday but AI'.
“there are a lot of categories where there never was an incumbent software company because the only job to be done was like actual human labor” ▶ 26:20

Lassie's onboarding is nearly self-serve: the doctor goes through a Stripe-like checkout or Replit-like flow linking the practice bank account, the system of record and the insurance portals, with configuration handled under the hood — modeled on how Robin Hood removed humans from brokerage account opening.
framework🗣 Steinconviction: highStripeReplitRobin HoodLassieinsurance portals

Paper-to-digital: the regulatory inflection 5
Stein says a federal mandate requiring the industry to stop paper checks and offer direct deposit to doctors — plus digital itemized invoice formats — is the regulatory inflection that makes this work automatable at all.
causal🗣 Steinconviction: highfederal governmentinsurance companiesdirect depositdental practices
So what: The automation opportunity is timing-dependent on a regulatory forcing function; similar mandates elsewhere would flag the next vertical.
“And then the federal government stepped in and they said this has to stop, right? You cannot do paper checks anymore for much longer.” ▶ 53:29
Stein estimates around 70% of these small businesses are still paid on paper and says the industry is going through a massive digitization revolution driven by that federal inflection point.
data_point🗣 Steinconviction: mediumsmall businessesinsurance companiespayments
So what: A quantified conversion runway; every practice flipped to digital deposits is a newly automatable account.
“I think the stats are 70% are still paid uh on paper and it's going through this massive like digitization revolution right now because federal inflection point that's regulatory” ▶ 53:55
Stein says even perfect models five years ago could not have done this job because the file cabinet was literally physical — he personally opened envelopes from the mailman and deposited $100,000 of paper checks with itemized invoices attached for Dr. Quan.
causal🗣 Steinconviction: highDr. Quanpaper checksLassie
So what: Model capability is not sufficient for vertical automation; data availability in digital form is the gating input to watch.
“and I kid you not, I open all these envelopes, deposit $100,000, like cash uh, in the bank account of the doctor” ▶ 52:59
Stein says the reason practices have not flipped to digital payments themselves is a 50-hour switching cost across Cigna, Delta and other carriers plus the problem that bank-account access would expose data staff should not see — so Lassie productized the conversion for them.
causal🗣 Steinconviction: highinsurance companiesDelta DentalLassiedental practices
So what: The conversion engine is a distinct, defensible product layer beneath the agent, and a barrier that a pure model-wrapper cannot replicate.
“Dr. loop needs to also then spend 50 hours figuring out with Sigma and Delta and all the other insurance companies, how do I flip the switch?” ▶ 54:29
Frederick argues payments stayed on paper partly because staff prefer working off physical sheets — digitizing without tools just yields a PDF that is harder to use — so there was no incentive to digitize until agents could act on the data.
causal🗣 Frederickconviction: mediumdental practicesPDFsdigitization
So what: Explains why digitization stalled for decades and why AI changes the incentive; a pattern to look for in other paper-bound industries.
“Part of the reason that uh all of these payments are still on paper is that the staff basically prefers doing the work by hand on paper.” ▶ 55:33

thesis🗣 Steinconviction: highinsurance companiesdental billing codesLassieAI agents
