causal🗣 Steinconviction: mediuminsurance companiesemployersdental practices
So what: Tempers the assumption that payer automation is purely adversarial; carriers may cooperate with agentic billing that reduces network friction.
Master plan, model limits and building a 2026 company 8
Lassie's stated end goal is that every small business runs itself, with a business agent interfacing with a consumer's personal agent and with agents at insurance companies and other counterparties.
prediction🗣 Steinconviction: highLassiesmall businessesAI agentsinsurance companies
So what: Agent-to-agent commerce as the terminal state implies infrastructure needs — identity, authorization, settlement — as an investable adjacency.
“every small business should run itself, right? And the busy work is done by uh agents” ▶ 34:10
Frederick says frontier models trained on vast data still do not know how to do this work because the workflows are not encoded anywhere — the knowledge sits in office managers' heads and is not accessible on the internet, so Lassie collects SOPs and infers workflows from customers' historical ERP data.
thesis🗣 Frederickconviction: highreasoning modelsLassieERPsSOPs
So what: Tacit-workflow data is the scarce input in vertical AI; companies with write access to historical operational data hold the real advantage over model access.
“The models are trained on so much data and they're so large and yet they actually don't really know how to do any of this work.” ▶ 0:18
Frederick names slow learning as the outstanding technical gap and says he is personally excited about smaller models that learn faster on less data, while acknowledging that might not benefit Lassie specifically.
prediction🗣 Frederickconviction: mediumsmaller modelsfoundation models
So what: A practitioner's signal that sample-efficient small models, not bigger frontier models, are the next unlock for vertical automation.
“I'm I'm personally kind of excited about uh smaller models that uh, you know, learn faster and can learn on less data” ▶ 48:05
Frederick says product building has shifted from shipping functionality to eliminating work: instead of building the ability to send a statement, the target is that the office spends zero further time on patient billing including the follow-up phone calls where the time actually goes.
framework🗣 Frederickconviction: highLassiepatient billingproduct management
So what: Reframes product scoping around time-and-motion analysis of where labor hours actually go, usually customer communication rather than transactions.
“once we deliver patient billing the office should not have to spend any more time on patient billing which is super different to giving them a tool that they then need to use” ▶ 38:57
Stein's contrarian hiring take is that nothing has changed on talent quality — you still need top-five-percentile engineers or sellers with steep slope — but Lassie now interviews specifically for how AI-pilled a candidate is across every function including finance.
contrarian🗣 Steinconviction: highLassiehiringAI adoption
So what: An operating template for 2026 org design: same talent bar, new screen for AI-native working assumptions in non-engineering roles.
“You still need people with steep slope um that like are very ambitious and and driven uh and have skills that are just like top five percentile either in engineering or in selling” ▶ 39:52
Stein says the goal is a 2026-version organization that ships twice as much as others and moves four times as fast, and calls the output-per-person multiple of four to five the big open experiment of company building right now.
prediction🗣 Steinconviction: mediumLassieAI-native organizations
So what: A falsifiable productivity claim to test against headcount and revenue disclosures at AI-native startups.
“we want to build a 2026 version of a of a of a big organization right uh where like we ship twice as much than others. We move like four times as like fast”
Alex poses the paradox that if AI makes small businesses far easier to start and run, competition could erode SMB margins — since the accumulation of people needed to deliver the service was itself the small business's moat.
contrarian🗣 Alexconviction: exploratorysmall businessesAI agentsmoats
So what: A downside scenario for the AI-for-SMB thesis: commoditized service supply could compress the very margins that fund the software.
“it could actually work out where like small businesses it's much easier to start one and run one but then actually paradoxically it's much harder to be one” ▶ 42:08
Stein rebuts the margin-erosion risk by arguing demand is not capped but supply-constrained — there is roughly twice as much dental and plumbing demand as can be supplied — so the outcome could be half a million dentists or the same dentists seeing twice as many patients.
thesis🗣 Steinconviction: highdental practicesplumbersprimary caresmall businesses
So what: If correct, AI expands service-sector volume rather than compressing price, which is bullish for SMB-serving vendors and for healthcare access volumes.
“this assumes there's a cap on kind of like demand and if you just look at the dental practice, but the same applies to try to find a good plumber” ▶ 43:34

Why BitMEX's Shutdown Got Nostalgia While Dead Tokens Get Rage: Uneasy Money72 min49 insights2026-07-31
▶ Watch on YouTube
Hosts Kane Wark and Taylor Monahan interview Offchain Labs CEO Stephen Goldfeder about the 2026 bear-market shutdown wave, arguing crypto has shifted from 'users buy your token' to hard revenue, and that L2 competition is now a proxy war between Robinhood chain (Arbitrum) and Base (Optimism) decided partly by licensing/business models. They dissect why BitMEX's shutdown produced nostalgia while tokenized shutdowns produce rage, a fresh wave of DPRK bridge hacks (Purpex/AFX misreported as an 'Arbitrum bridge' hack), Uniswap's permissioned pools with Securitize/Superstate as a move toward rights-bearing pseudo-equity tokens, Arbitrum DAO governance fixes, and Kyle Samani's public break with Multicoin over Hyperliquid.
Bear-market die-off: revenue replaces token price as the lifeline · 7L2 proxy wars: Robinhood chain vs Base, and licensing as the moat · 6Why token failures draw rage and equity failures draw nostalgia · 6Bridge hacks: DPRK crews, AI offense, and forgotten discipline · 8Permissioned DeFi and the pseudo-equity token experiment · 11Arbitrum DAO governance: apathy, delegates and the security council · 6Kyle Samani vs Multicoin and Solana's single-patron problem · 5
Bear-market die-off: revenue replaces token price as the lifeline 7
Crypto project shutdown announcements are running at roughly ten a week less than six months into the 2026 bear market, far earlier in the cycle than in prior downturns, because it has become socially acceptable to capitulate rather than grind.
data_point🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: mediumcrypto startupsbear market
So what: An early, compressed capitulation wave means distressed acqui-hire and asset-purchase opportunities arrive sooner than the usual 18-24 month bear-market bottom playbook implies.
“we're only like we're less than six months into this bare market, right? So, it's not like we're we're two years deep.” ▶ 2:09
Founders shutting down in 2026 increasingly have live users but no monetization, meaning the failure mode is business-model failure rather than product failure.
causal🗣 Stephen Goldfeder, co-founder/CEO Offchain Labsconviction: highArbitrumOffchain Labscrypto startups
So what: Screen portfolio and ecosystem projects on revenue per user, not TVL or DAU — usage metrics no longer predict survival.
“it's like yeah we have users we just we just we don't make any money off” ▶ 4:32
The old crypto business model was that users paid teams indirectly by buying the token, so token appreciation substituted for revenue; that channel is now closed and revenue has become a mandatory word.
framework🗣 Kane Wark and Stephen Goldfederconviction: hightokenscrypto startups
So what: Reprice any token whose implicit cash flow was 'users buy the token' — that demand function is structurally broken, not cyclically weak.
“whereas back in the day having users was sufficient for the token price like your users would buy the token that was how they paid you.” ▶ 4:39
Crypto's cyclicality forces teams to compete unsustainably in bull markets, so companies that raised what would be a two-to-three-year Series A runway burn it in about one year and die.
causal🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highventure capitalcrypto startups
So what: Underwrite crypto Series A burn at roughly 2-3x the equivalent SaaS rate when modelling default-dead dates for 2024-2025 vintage rounds.
“teams that raised what would in a normal uh like ecosystem be like a three-year runway, right, in like a series A or something like that, like you know, two to three years burn through all their cash in like a year” ▶ 5:42
Arbitrum and other ecosystems have shifted from grants toward a sustainable investment model, and Offchain Labs explicitly will not prop up products that are not working.
thesis🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highArbitrumOffchain Labsecosystem grants
So what: Ecosystem-subsidy revenue lines in L2 app budgets should be marked down; grant-dependent projects face a funding cliff.
“I don't think there's any like benefit in like propping up negative bad protocols.” ▶ 7:49
Offchain Labs' grant test is that every dollar given out must have a credible story for two dollars returning to the Arbitrum ecosystem, in contrast to the logo-chasing model of paying everyone today and hoping they pay you next year.
framework🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highArbitrumOffchain Labs
So what: A concrete, reusable ROI filter for evaluating whether an L1/L2's incentive program is building durable share or just renting TVL.
“for every dollar that we give out we should have a story of how $2 come back to the arbitrary ecosystem” ▶ 8:38
Consolidation in crypto is coalescing around exactly two attributes — real distribution partners with real users (Coinbase, Robinhood) and solid technology — and the winners are those where the two combine.
framework🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highCoinbaseRobin HoodArbitrum
So what: Use distribution-plus-tech as the survivorship screen for infrastructure bets; projects with neither are the shutdown candidates.
“consolidation I would say around two things around uh solid distribution um that's like really really big whether it's Coinbase or Robin Hood but like real distribution partners with real users” ▶ 9:24
L2 proxy wars: Robinhood chain vs Base, and licensing as the moat 6
Arbitrum and Optimism are no longer competing head-to-head as chains but through corporate proxies — Robinhood chain on the Arbitrum side and Base on the Optimism side — with the L2 teams supplying technology rather than fighting on the front lines.
framework🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highArbitrumOptimismRobin HoodBaseCoinbase
So what: Value in the rollup stack accrues to whoever captures the distribution partner; track L2 competitive position by partner franchise, not by native chain TVL.

Offchain Labs' Business Source License, initially criticized, is now the core structural difference from Optimism because Coinbase and Base could choose to simply stop paying OP whereas large partners must contractually engage with Arbitrum.
thesis🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highOffchain LabsArbitrumOptimismCoinbaseBaseUniswapBusiness Source License
So what: Licensing terms, not decentralization rhetoric, determine whether an L2 stack can monetize its biggest deployments — a durable revenue-capture differentiator to underwrite.

Giving away open-source infrastructure for free without a licensing hook invites large capital-backed users to fork and cut the original team out, which the host frames as inviting capitalists into a communist utopia.
causal🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highOptimismopen sourceCoinbase
So what: For any open-source infra investment, ask what forces the largest deployer to keep paying; without it, revenue is voluntary.

Arbitrum deliberately does not build first-party apps — no Arbitrum DEX, no first-party tokenized assets — so builders are not competing against the house, which Goldfeder frames as a structural differentiator versus other L2s.
thesis🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highArbitrumOffchain LabsUniswapL2s
So what: Neutrality at the app layer is a recruiting advantage for L2s; watch whether competitors that vertically integrate lose third-party builders.
“we don't really get involved at the app layer. So you know you're not playing against us.” ▶ 7:18
Offchain Labs runs a deliberate dual strategy in which Robinhood chain's success and Arbitrum One's own DeFi adoption are both top priorities, because relying purely on proxies is high-risk given proxies have their own agendas.
thesis🗣 Stephen Goldfeder and Kane Warkconviction: highArbitrumRobin HoodOffchain LabsDeFi

Why token failures draw rage and equity failures draw nostalgia 6
BitMEX's shutdown produced nostalgia rather than anger specifically because it never issued a token, so no retail spectators lost money on it despite almost every early trader using the platform.
causal🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highBitMEXtokenscrypto traders
So what: Community sentiment risk is a function of token issuance, not product quality — a real reputational cost to tokenizing that founders should price in.
“It's sad that they're shutting down, but people aren't angry about it.” ▶ 16:41
Token holders feel entitled to viciously post-mortem a failed token project even though a token holder's actual influence over a project is essentially identical to a customer's influence over BitMEX.
contrarian🗣 Taylor Monahan, co-hostconviction: mediumtokensBitMEX
So what: The perceived governance rights of most tokens are illusory; this gap is exactly what rights-bearing token designs are trying to close.
“the influence as a token holder or the influence you have over a token project versus Bitmax is essentially the same.” ▶ 24:25
Because token projects are attention-driven and token purchases substitute for revenue, they tend either to become long-term zombies or to grind to zero and quietly shut the labs entity, so observers are often surprised the project was still alive.
framework🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: hightokenscrypto startups
So what: Distinguish zombie tokens (dead economics, live ticker) from operating businesses; zombie supply overhangs can persist for years before the formal wind-down.
“token projects tend to be these like you know oftent times uh like things that either go long-term zombie right or like they burn through all their cash and then you just they kind of disappear they grind to zero” ▶ 19:27
BitMEX was the consensus 2015 pick for a top-ten crypto company of 2030 — ahead of a then-nonexistent Binance — and it still failed, demonstrating that even maximal early advantage, revenue and tailwinds do not prevent startup failure in crypto.
anecdote🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highBitMEXBinanceCoinbaseKrakenBitfinexPoloniexBittrex
So what: Base-rate discipline: incumbency and profitability in crypto exchanges are weak predictors of ten-year survival — size positions accordingly.
“if you went back to 2015 and said like what are the startups that will be like the kings of crypto in in 2030, everyone would have said BitMX.” ▶ 22:26
BitMEX's original model — no KYC, a Bitcoin-funded database with a troll box, treated as sufficiently decentralized because deposits were in Bitcoin — is unrepeatable today, as later attempts by Nat Alnaji showed.
causal🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highBitMEXArthur HayesNat AlnajiKYC
So what: Regulatory arbitrage that created the last generation of exchange fortunes is closed; new entrants must underwrite compliance cost from day one.
“being powered by Bitcoin was theme in 2014 is like sufficiently decentralized, right?” ▶ 20:49
The irony of the rage asymmetry is that token projects were structurally far less sustainable than BitMEX, yet holders of tokens with no connection to revenue are the ones shocked when the project fails.
contrarian🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highBitMEXPoloniextokens
So what: Sentiment is not a signal of underlying business quality; the loudest post-mortems cluster on the weakest business models.
“bro like you bought a token that had no connection to anything or revenues or anything and you're like shocked and appalled that like it didn't make it” ▶ 25:12
Bridge hacks: DPRK crews, AI offense, and forgotten discipline 8
Four or five bridge hacks occurred in a recent short window with many more over the past month, reversing a multi-year period in which bridges felt safe and were no longer being drained daily.
data_point🗣 Taylor Monahan, co-hostconviction: highbridgesDeFi security
So what: Cross-chain bridge TVL is being repriced for risk again; insurance costs and bridge-dependent yield strategies should be re-underwritten.
“we had so many bridges and it's not even last week. I think there were four or five, but in the past month there have been many.” ▶ 26:49
A widely circulated headline that 'the Arbitrum bridge has been hacked' was actually a third-party Purpex/AFX bridge only about a month and a half old, and Offchain Labs had to correct the narrative fast to avoid panic-driven secondary effects.
anecdote🗣 Stephen Goldfeder and Taylor Monahanconviction: highArbitrumPurpex AFXOffchain Labsbridges
So what: Automated hack-alert feeds and AI-written headlines create false attribution risk that can move sentiment on the wrong asset — a tradable mispricing window and a real operational risk for L2s.
“we activated very quickly. Uh we uh confirmed that it wasn't the arbitrum bridge. We uh figured out whose bridge it was.” ▶ 29:40
The Purpex/AFX bridge exploit involved five compromised hot validator signing keys and has been attributed to the same DPRK crew behind the LayerZero and Ronin hacks, who can extract keys even when they are split across separate servers.
data_point🗣 Taylor Monahan, co-hostconviction: highPurpex AFXLayerZeroRoninDPRKHyperliquid
So what: Multisig key-splitting across servers is not a sufficient control against this actor; bridges relying on hot validator signers are a persistent target class.
“five five compromised hot validator signing keys.” ▶ 31:42
AI has broken the human-versus-human security equilibrium that held bridge attackers at bay for roughly two to three years, and defenders have not yet marshaled equivalent tooling despite new open-source security agents and models now permitted to do cybersecurity work.
thesis🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highAICodexK3Qwen Coder 3.8DeFi security
So what: Expect elevated exploit frequency until defensive AI tooling catches up — a window favoring shorter-duration and lower-TVL cross-chain exposure and favoring AI-security vendors.
“we were able to like, you know, find an equilibrium where like the best humans were able to keep the other best humans at bay for a period of like 2 3 years.” ▶ 33:25
Offchain Labs' historical bridge-risk mitigation was hardening the native bridge plus an education campaign pushing large exchanges off immediate sequencer-only confirmed withdrawals toward waiting for L1 finality or setting explicit size thresholds.
framework🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highArbitrumOffchain LabsexchangesEthereum
So what: A concrete operational control to check when assessing any exchange's L2 withdrawal policy — sequencer-only confirmation is an unhedged trust assumption.
“initially actually many of the large exchanges were doing like immediate sequencer only confirmed withdrawals and we did a campaign that you should wait until it lands, you know, gets L1 finality” ▶ 35:02
Offchain Labs does not audit third-party bridges deployed on Arbitrum and only performs second-level diligence on whether the team looks legitimate, so ecosystem bridge risk sits largely outside the L2's control.
data_point🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highArbitrumOffchain Labsbridges
So what: Do not treat an L2's endorsement or ecosystem listing as a security signal for a bridge; diligence must be done independently.
“we don't audit their code but we try to check out are these like legitimate people” ▶ 36:02
Demand for security is cyclical: in good times users only ask for features and the lowest fees, and only an FTX-scale event resets the appetite for decentralization and security guarantees.
framework🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highFTXL2ssidechainsDeFi security
So what: Security-differentiated infrastructure is structurally undervalued in bull markets and rerates after blowups — a repeatable timing pattern for infra positioning.
“but like in good times times no one like hears about security which they just say like give me the features give me the lowest fees” ▶ 40:11
The industry successfully beat 'don't roll your own crypto' into engineers but never established the equivalent norm against rolling your own bridge, even though a self-built bridge carrying enormous value is obviously absurd by real-world analogy.
framework🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highbridgesL2scryptography

“there was not yet this sentiment of like you can build your own bridge” ▶ 37:39
Permissioned DeFi and the pseudo-equity token experiment 11
Uniswap Labs launched permissioned pools with Securitize, Superstate and Dalgo that embed the compliance check inside the V4 pool contract itself, letting tokenized-fund issuers restrict trading to approved investors without a centralized gatekeeper standing in front of the market.
data_point🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highUniswapSecuritizeSuperstateDalgotokenized securities
So what: Moves tokenization's compliance layer from app to protocol, which is the missing plumbing for regulated assets to trade on public AMMs — watch adoption as the gating factor for RWA volume.
“until now compliance for tokenized securities lived at the app layer. A gate standing in front of the market permission pools move those rules into the pool itself. that's the piece of plumbing tokenization has been missing.” ▶ 41:23
Superstate's core thesis, per host and investor Kane Wark, is to replace rights-less tokens with instruments carrying some or all of the properties of equity — investor rights, disclosures, ties to revenue — with the unavoidable cost that fewer people are legally allowed to hold them.
thesis🗣 Kane Wark, host and Superstate investorconviction: highSuperstateRobert Leshnertokenized securitiesequity
So what: If pseudo-equity tokens gain traction, rights-less governance tokens face a quality-competition problem they cannot answer without restructuring.
“let's not have these um assets called tokens that have no rights um no obligations no uh you know ties to uh revenue” ▶ 42:53
The host predicts Superstate will break out and that some big projects will choose the pseudo-equity token structure this cycle, producing the first real-time natural experiment on whether markets prefer rights with restrictions over open access without rights.
prediction🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highSuperstatetokensequity
So what: A concrete, observable 2026 catalyst: track which large protocols adopt a rights-bearing structure and how their tokens trade versus rights-less comparables.
“I think Superstate is going to uh like kind of break out and we'll see some big projects make this decision. So, we will get to see this experiment in real time.” ▶ 1:01:59
The contrarian defense of permissioned DeFi is that controls are the precondition for rights: with literally no controls you get a free-for-all PvP market where holders have no rights, no upside claim and no information about what they bought.
contrarian🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highUniswappermissioned poolsDeFitokens
So what: Reframes the compliance debate as a rights trade rather than an ideological loss — the argument that will be used to sell restricted instruments to crypto-native holders.
“If there are literally no controls, if there's literally no nothing, you can't have either way. It's just a free-for-all. It's just PvP.” ▶ 0:10
A definable segment of sophisticated capital — endowment-type investors — will not buy rights-less tokens and defaults to buying equity instead, meaning permissionless token design has an implicit ceiling on institutional participation.
thesis🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highHarvard endowmentinstitutional investorsArbitrumOffchain Labstokens
So what: Rights-bearing structures are the mechanism to unlock a buyer pool currently absent from token markets — the marginal-buyer argument for tokenized equity.
“if you are the Harvard endowment like go and buy equity in a thing if you're excited about it. Don't buy the magic beans that the guy's selling” ▶ 58:57
Prevailing token designs — labs corp, foundation, DAO, token holder splits — were a function of the Gensler era and legal uncertainty rather than genuine preference, and are now being revisited as clarity improves.
causal🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highGary GenslerSECDAOsfoundationstokens
So what: Expect a wave of token/entity restructurings as legal constraints loosen; current structures are not equilibrium designs.
“a lot of the like token designs that were like popularized were very much a function of of the Gensler era” ▶ 49:14
There was an incentive incompatibility in the prior regime where no individual team would take the legal risk of innovating on token structure, so everyone copied the same paved-path formula even without SEC blessing.
framework🗣 Kane Wark and Stephen Goldfederconviction: highSECventure capitaltokens
So what: Explains why token design stagnated and why a single credible first mover (e.g. Superstate) can unlock a rapid design shift.
“there was like an incentive like incompatibility right where like no individual entity was going to take that risk.” ▶ 50:17
Goldfeder expects the CLARITY legislation to function as a platform for rulemaking and deeper conversations about decentralization and ownership limits rather than instant answers, with real market effects a year or two after passage.
prediction🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: mediumCLARITY ActDeFiregulation

“even if clarity passes tomorrow I think” ▶ 1:04:47
Goldfeder would not trade Arbitrum's ~97% globally-eligible holder base for a 5% restricted base, because the censorship-resistance question is how easily a small, concentrated (largely New York-domiciled) holder set could be compelled to act.
thesis🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highArbitrumOffchain Labsgovernancetokens
So what: The strongest counterargument to pseudo-equity tokens for chains: broad distribution is a security property, not just a liquidity property.
“how many people where are these, you know, where are these five doiciled and how easy would it be to compel the five to do something” ▶ 1:01:25
Offchain Labs turned down seed investors in 2017-2018 who would only invest if Arbitrum issued a token; the token ultimately came from the need for governance over mutable contracts, with revenue routing to the token added later.

Chains are the main exception to the rights-less-token critique because they have core functionality that gives the token value without investor rights, whereas an application protocol like Compound would be a less controversial candidate for an equity-like instrument.
framework🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: mediumArbitrumCompoundtokensL1s

Arbitrum DAO governance: apathy, delegates and the security council 6
Offchain Labs' self-identified early mistake was over-emphasizing decentralization by conflating the root of power with operational logistics, so the DAO ended up voting on trivia and smart delegates disengaged out of apathy.
framework🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highArbitrumArbitrum DAOOffchain Labsgovernance
So what: A reusable governance design principle — separate root-of-power decisions from operations — for evaluating whether a DAO can retain competent participants.
“Like the DAO should not be voting what brand of toilet paper to use at the arbitrum.” ▶ 55:22
Arbitrum's governance fixes were to eliminate professional delegate programs, put votes live on a fixed Thursday schedule so participants know when to look, and delegate operational decisions to the OpCo operating company via a vote.
data_point🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highArbitrum DAOArbitrum OpCogovernance
So what: Concrete, copyable governance mechanics; also a signal of DAO maturation that reduces the tail risk of low-turnout capture.
“So, what we did was we got rid of these professional delegate programs. We we uh put in a set of procedures where votes go live now on Thursday”
Offchain Labs prohibits itself and its employees from voting their Arbitrum tokens directly in order to preserve space for community delegates.
data_point🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highOffchain LabsArbitrum DAOgovernance
So what: Materially changes the effective voting float in Arbitrum governance — relevant when modelling proposal outcomes or activist campaigns.
“we for example on offchain don't allow uh ourselves or employees to directly vote uh with their tokens” ▶ 54:20
The Arbitrum security council's intervention during the Kelp hack was, per Goldfeder, proof that a broadly elected external entity — not the founding team — can hold emergency upgrade power, with the token serving as the instrument that elects it.
thesis🗣 Stephen Goldfederconviction: highArbitrumArbitrum Security CouncilKelpgovernance

“it was by design that there was this external entity right that's like you know broadly elected and it wasn't like me that had the power to do that” ▶ 47:09
Governance apathy is an exploitable attack surface, as shown by roughly ten BarnBridge-style incidents where a vote passed unnoticed and the treasury was drained.
causal🗣 Taylor Monahan and Stephen Goldfederconviction: mediumBarnBridgeDAOsgovernance
So what: Low-participation DAOs with sizable treasuries are a live risk category; monitor quorum ratios as a security metric.
“that's the problem where sometimes just people aren't paying attention.” ▶ 55:04
Delegating heavy power to an elected council works only when paired with an instantaneous recall mechanism that lets holders dissolve the council if it misbehaves, per Kane Wark's experience with Synthetix.
framework🗣 Kane Wark, hostconviction: highSynthetixDAOsgovernance
So what: Recall speed, not delegation depth, is the control that makes council governance safe — a diligence checkpoint for any DAO structure.
“we had other checks where like you could vote them off instantaneously” ▶ 56:40

Natalie Brunell 1 episode(s) · 49 insights

Jack Mallers on Leaving 21: "Now I Can Tell You Whatever You Want"66 min49 insights2026-07-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Jack Mallers, freshly resigned from Bitcoin treasury company 21 (co-founded with Tether, second-largest corporate BTC treasury at 43,514 BTC) and no longer bound by public-company disclosure rules, explains that he walked away from a ~3% stake worth hundreds of millions rather than fold Strike into 21 after investors pressured the board to consolidate. He argues 2025 was never a real bull market (Bitcoin never made new highs versus gold), that Bitcoin is a hybrid of disruptive technology plus expectations of future fiat liquidity, and that genuine Bitcoin understanding is closer to 800,000 people globally — a basis point — leaving room for a $10-20 trillion asset before 1% adoption.
Why 21 was built and why Mallers walked · 9Treasury-company mechanics and the window that closed · 9Cycle read: 2025 was not a real bull market · 10Strike's economics and product roadmap · 7Adoption math and the self-custody debate · 8Bitcoin as an uncorruptible truth mechanism · 6
Why 21 was built and why Mallers walked 9
Jack Mallers co-founded Bitcoin treasury company 21 with Tether — specifically chairman Giancarlo Devasini and CEO Paolo Ardoino — and says Tether constitutes the board, meaning he never controlled the company he named, ticker-registered and logo-designed.
data_point🗣 Jack Mallersconviction: high21TetherPaolo ArdoinoJack Mallers
So what: Governance, not operations, was the binding constraint at 21 — investors in Tether-backed vehicles should assume Tether's board controls strategy regardless of who fronts the brand.
“21 was never mine. Um it was always up to the board. Uh Tether makes up the board and so at a certain point we began to disagree” ▶ 6:22
Mallers frames the Bitcoin-equity landscape as a spectrum with Coinbase and Robinhood at one end (customers, cash flow, products, DCF-valuable but low Bitcoin conviction) and Strategy/Michael Sailor at the other (maximum Bitcoin conviction but self-described capital markets financial engineering with no products or cash flow), with 21 designed as the missing middle.
framework🗣 Jack Mallersconviction: highCoinbaseRobinhoodStrategyMichael Sailor21
So what: A reusable lens for screening crypto equities: score each name on conviction versus cash-flow durability, and treat 'middle' vehicles as unproven rather than automatically superior.
“can we build a business that has products and cash flow and growth prospects whilst also displaying an immense amount of singular focus conviction in this Bitcoin thing?” ▶ 5:33
Mallers reads Coinbase's balance sheet as roughly $10-11 billion of assets with less than $1 billion in Bitcoin, calling that under 10% conviction in Bitcoin expressed monetarily.
data_point🗣 Jack Mallersconviction: highCoinbaseBitcoinEthereum
So what: Balance-sheet composition is a cheap proxy for where a listed crypto operator's real incentives sit — useful when picking between Bitcoin-levered and altcoin-levered equity exposure.
“if you look at their balance sheet, they've got 10 11 billion and less than one is Bitcoin” ▶ 4:23
Mallers says the original founding agreement explicitly excluded folding Strike into 21, but investors later lobbied the board to have him consolidate the two because they could not tell which company his announcements and keynotes applied to.
anecdote🗣 Jack Mallersconviction: highStrike21Tether
So what: Founder-brand overlap across a private company and a public vehicle is a live conflict risk that markets will eventually force to resolve — usually against the private company's independence.
“the market wants Jack to consolidate like when Jack announces something or does a keynote is is he talking about the thing that I bought or like what's the difference” ▶ 13:44
Mallers forfeited an approximately 3% ownership stake in 21 — which he estimates at hundreds of millions of dollars given a multibillion-dollar company valuation — rather than sell or merge Strike.
data_point🗣 Jack Mallersconviction: high21StrikeJack Mallers
So what: Quantifies the founder's revealed preference: Strike's independence was priced above nine figures, a signal about how he will resist future acquisition or public-listing pressure.
“I was 3% owner of 21, which I no longer” ▶ 31:37
Mallers describes the departure decision as a blank-check buyout offer that he rejected instinctively before reasoning it through, framing it as a test of stated principles rather than a strategic calculation.
anecdote🗣 Jack Mallersconviction: highJack MallersStrike21
So what: Sets expectations that Strike will not be sold or taken public on financial terms alone — relevant to anyone modeling a Strike exit or secondary.
“here's a blank check and here's what you anything you want. Like, are you willing to to sell out? And you know, it was interesting is before I could even like reason it out, I found myself like I just couldn't. There's just no way.”
Mallers explicitly calls his 21 tenure a failure because he communicated a vision and set public expectations he could not meet, while insisting the company itself has not failed and retains the second-largest corporate treasury, NYSE listing and a capable board.
thesis🗣 Jack Mallersconviction: high21New York Stock ExchangeJack Mallers
So what: Separates founder-departure headline risk from underlying asset quality — 21 holders are being told the balance sheet is intact even as the founder narrative collapses.
“I communicated a vision and I communicated expectations that weren't met and I I need to own that” ▶ 24:59
Mallers says he is legally free to speak now that he is no longer a public company officer reporting to the SEC, and that being a public-company officer was excruciatingly painful and constrained how he could communicate with Bitcoiners.

Treasury-company mechanics and the window that closed 9
21 launched in April 2025 as the first treasury company of this era — ahead of Adam Back's Nakamoto — into a market Mallers describes as uncrowded, with liquidity and exuberance, conditions that no longer exist.
data_point🗣 Jack Mallersconviction: high21NakamotoAdam Back
